rowspan="1">5</td></tr></tbody></table></figure><h2>Testimonials</h2><p>I’m not able to share the individual free-write responses from the survey above, but I recently personally asked some individuals who I think are doing high-impact work to tell me how they came to be doing that work, followed by what they thought the most important or counterfactual influences on their trajectories were.</p><p>Below, I include Claude summaries of their overall stories along with their description of the most important influences, lightly edited. Some notes on the testimonials I've included:</p><ul><li>They're obviously to some extent cherry-picked by me, and are meant to give a flavor of the kind of data we've seen, rather than a faithful representation of all the ways people tend to come to be doing this work.</li><li>I chose to include individuals who started doing AI safety-relevant work relatively recently (within the last 5 years), but who I think are doing at least somewhat legibly impactful work now. This includes many people who got involved in 2022 or earlier, and similar to the survey data above, I would advise against directly extrapolating the effectiveness of the exact influences they discuss from that time period, though I think the broad classes of influences (university and local groups, certain content and works of writing, programs and events) continue to be very impactful today– see <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#What_tends_to_work_">below</a>.<ul><li>Among other effects, I think prior to 2023, many more people doing impactful work in the AI safety space got involved via effective altruism rather than directly via AI safety-- nowadays, I think it's more common that people encounter AI safety right away.</li></ul></li></ul><h3><a href="https://www.linkedin.com/in/neel-nanda%F0%9F%94%B8-993580151/">Neel Nanda</a> (Senior Research Scientist at Google DeepMind)</h3><p>“Here's a list of the salient influences on me:</p><ul><li>When I was 14, I read <strong>Harry Potter and the Methods of Rationality</strong>, and via this came across LessWrong and Effective Altruism, and got generally curious about these spaces.</li><li>When I was 17-18, going to <strong>ESPR</strong>, absorbing a bunch of ideas about ambition, agency, thinking more clearly, and getting various in-person ties to the community.</li><li><strong>At uni [Cambridge], hanging out with the Effective Altruism group</strong>, forming friends via it. Meeting the EA community, especially outside of Cambridge, and generally absorbing the culture more and getting more of a sense of "maybe I should do something about this career-wise."<ul><li>By default, I was pretty sure I was going to go into finance while being highly uncertain, and considered AI safety kind of weird and vague.</li></ul></li><li>I had a call with <strong>80,000 Hours</strong> in my second year of uni (out of a three-year degree), where my main two updates were:<ul><li>I was being way too perfectionist about AI safety careers, and I should just try to gather information rather than trying to figure out if I was highly confident I wanted to do this for the rest of my life.</li><li>Also, connections with various people actually working on this stuff, including empirical work at labs, which gave me a much crisper sense of what this could actually look like. (I actually already knew some of the people I was connected to, just lacked the agency/inspiration to reach out myself)</li></ul></li><li>After I finished my undergrad, I was planning on doing a master's, but this was in 2020, so that was pretty unappealing because of COVID. I came close to accepting a Jane Street full-time offer, but instead decided I was being too risk-averse and I should instead gather information by doing a year of back-to-back AI safety internships.</li><li>I then <strong>interned</strong> at FHI doing AI safety theory stuff, which was kind of useless impact wise. Then DeepMind doing some empirical but not particularly impactful work - this was high value for just giving me a much more legible sense of "this is a career path" and feeling more like a tangible thing that I could learn and do, and where I was learning real skills. And CHAI, which was also a bit of a mess due to being remote + 8 hour time difference, and not being a great fit.<ul><li>I think the key salient updates from this year were just thinking a lot more about AI safety, talking to real people in the space, and getting a much more visceral sense of "something big is happening here, I can be involved, I can help, and I have realistic job options."</li></ul></li><li>Then I got a job offer from Anthropic, decided to accept, had a fantastic mentor in Chris Olah and discovered that mech interp was a great fit, and from then on it was pretty overdetermined that I was going to stay in the AI safety space.”</li></ul><h3><a href="https://www.linkedin.com/in/max-nadeau/">Max Nadeau</a> (Associate Program Officer (Technical AI Safety) at Coefficient Giving)</h3><p><strong>Claude’s summary:</strong></p><p>Max got it into his head in high school that human-level AI was coming during his lifetime and that it was important to make sure the process went well, but he had no idea anyone was working on it. In college, he got connected with Stephen Casper, where he learned practical ML skills, and to someone who connected him to the people running the Impact Generator retreat [Asya note: this was a small GCR-focused workshop series run in the Bay in 2022], which he was later invited to. He talked to Tao Lin at that retreat, and Tao offered him a TA position at the ML bootcamp Redwood was running, with three weeks to learn the material. He thought he'd be in the Bay for three days, but stayed six weeks. TA'ing turned into an internship at Redwood, which he took a semester off college to do. While interning he got to know Ajeya, and by the time he graduated she offered him a job.</p><p><strong>Max on what was most important:</strong></p><ul><li>"Going to the <strong>Impact Generator workshop</strong>. That was, like, extremely random. And, like, resulted in a major acceleration to my career."</li><li>"I think, like, <strong>getting connected with the existing AI safety community [at Harvard] in the first place</strong> was really counterfactual. I went from, like, having some vague sense of, like, maybe this is something I want to do, to oh, there are already people working on this issue, and they have a whole way of thinking about it."</li></ul><h3><a href="https://www.linkedin.com/in/rachel-weinberg-789b23228/">Rachel Weinberg</a> (founder and former head of The Curve, currently at AI Futures Project)</h3><p><strong>Claude’s summary:</strong></p><p>Rachel got into effective altruism in high school through friends, and started a group at her university. She spent some time interning running retreats and ended-up helping with Future Forum, a futurism conference that required a last-minute venue switch. She took a semester off to study AI safety, but decided she wasn't interested in research, and did web dev for a while. After running Manifest 2024, she started The Curve, and is now working on other field-building projects.</p><p><strong>Rachel on what was most important:</strong></p><ul><li>“(obviously) <strong>Getting into effective altruism in the first place via friends</strong> (Nick Gabrieli in particular).</li><li><strong>Helping with Future Forum</strong>, which came from meeting Leilani at Impact Generator [Asya note: this was a small GCR-focused workshop series run in the Bay in 2022] and then from her agreeing to take on the project last minute (which I encouraged at the time, but some people were against). I at least benefited a lot from being on a small, messy team where it was easier to stand out by being eager to take on more responsibility and following through.</li><li><strong>Deciding to run The Curve</strong>, which was largely dependent on my ~personality (tbh Austin gets counterfactual credit for pushing my confidence/conviction to the threshold of being willing to take that kind of risk), but was also very inspired by having seen Future Forum bootstrap. I also probably wouldn't have been/felt qualified to do this if I didn't live in the Bay, maybe SF in particular.”</li></ul><h3><a href="https://www.linkedin.com/in/marius-hobbhahn-128927175/">Marius Hobbhann</a> (CEO and founder of Apollo Research)</h3><p><strong>Claude’s summary:</strong></p><p>During his first week of university in 2015, someone handed him Superintelligence. He studied cognitive science, did a CS bachelor's in parallel, then a machine learning master's and PhD to prepare for AI safety work. In 2022 he started doing AI safety research on the side with a grant from the Long-Term Future Fund. He paused his PhD, did MATS in early 2023, concluded that deceptive alignment was the biggest problem and that no one was doing evals for it, and started Apollo, which he’s been running since.</p><p><strong>Marius on what was most important:</strong></p><ul><li>"In the early days, I would say it was <strong>people who were already considering AI safety back then</strong>. These were the kind of people I  looked up to and who also nudged me towards doing AI safety because they also thought it would be super important.</li><li><strong>The personal grants</strong> were super important for me too. Because it basically meant there was no excuse anymore. I really wanted to do work on AI safety, but there were always questions like:  Is this financially responsible? And what about having a stable career? And at the point where you have a grant, these concerns didn’t seem like a big deal anymore. And it also was a motivational boost, of course, because someone else thought I’m good enough to bet some money on.</li><li><strong>MATS</strong> was very impactful. Apollo definitely wouldn't exist without MATS. That’s where I had the time to develop an agenda, do a lot of experimentation and find great starting members.</li><li>There were also just <strong>a bunch of people in the Bay</strong> who I talked to who suggested it would be a good idea to start Apollo and give it a try, even if there's a good chance that it doesn't work out.  For example, Evan Hubinger, my MATS mentor, was supportive and helpful.</li><li>Oh, and then <strong>the AI safety philanthropic ecosystem</strong>. That’s how we received our starting capital and which allowed us to try Apollo.</li></ul><h3><a href="https://www.linkedin.com/in/adam-r-kaufman/">Adam Kaufman</a> (member of technical staff at Redwood Research)</h3><p><strong>Claude’s summary:</strong></p><p>Adam knew from an early age that superintelligence would be scary if someone built it, but assumed it wasn't going to happen in his lifetime. When he got to college, he joined the AI Safety Fundamentals reading group that the Harvard AI Safety group (HAIST) was running, thought the people were extremely cool, and made most of his close friends there. He became increasingly convinced the problem was urgent as language models kept getting smarter. He met Buck Shlegeris at a HAIST retreat, talked to him, and applied to MATS. He did MATS at Redwood, enjoyed it so much he took time off school, and has been working there since.<br /><br />Adam on what was most important:</p><ul><li>“Definitely I think that being around a community of smart people who were planning to do careers in AI safety, were convinced that this was a really important problem, was probably necessary for getting me to like seriously consider that I should work on this myself. [...] I think <strong>HAIST (the Harvard AI Safety Team)</strong> was probably fairly counterfactual for me. It's like if that club didn't exist, I imagine I would have just been more depressed and confused about what I should do."</li><li>"I think that <strong>talking to Buck in a hot tub once [at the HAIST/MAIA retreat]</strong> was probably counterfactual for getting my current job."</li><li>"Definitely having the opportunity to <strong>intern at Redwood [through MATS or otherwise]</strong> was necessary for me [taking a full-time job there]."</li></ul><h3><a href="https://www.linkedin.com/in/gabriel-d-wu/">Gabriel Wu</a> (member of technical staff (alignment) at OpenAI)</h3><p><strong>Claude’s summary:</strong></p><p>Gabe was given a copy of The Precipice when he started as a freshman at Harvard. There was no formal AI safety team at the time, but a group of 7-10 people would gather weekly to talk about x-risk in a dining hall, so he joined, and ended up going to a long workshop in Orinda [California]. He did REMIX [Asya note: this was a mechanistic interpretability bootcamp] the following winter, which introduced him to the Constellation community, and then applied for a Redwood internship for the next summer. After others graduated, he became the new director of HAIST (the Harvard AI Safety Team). He worked with the Alignment Research Center, applied to labs, and was eventually convinced by several people to join OpenAI.</p><p><strong>Gabe on what was most important:</strong></p><ul><li>"I think a huge part of it was being identified by [<strong>students at HAIST (the Harvard AI Safety Team)</strong>]. They made sure to bump me to apply to things. It really felt like they believed in me and wanted to make sure that I didn't get lost along the way. And I think that was pretty counterfactual because it made me a lot more likely to end up doing REMIX and so on. [...] The fact that <strong>HAIST</strong> itself existed was a big part of it."</li><li>"Another thing I mentioned was just like, having the opportunity to visit <strong>Constellation.</strong>"</li></ul><h3><a href="https://www.linkedin.com/in/ce-brewer/">Catherine Brewer</a> (Senior Program Associate (AI Governance) at Coefficient Giving)</h3><p><strong>Claude’s summary:</strong></p><p>Catherine found 80,000 Hours before university through internet searching about careers, then read Doing Good Better. They engaged with the Oxford effective altruism university group, going to events and helping run programming. Through the group they made friends who were into AI safety and argued with them a bunch, which got them interested in AI safety. They applied for the ERA fellowship (then called CERI) after someone from the group told them to, and spent a summer thinking about AI safety with other people. Then they did the GovAI fellowship, which they found even more helpful, via meeting people and developing her own takes on relevant topics. After that they were interested in AI governance, and applied to Open Philanthropy when they were graduating.</p><p><strong>Catherine on what was most important:</strong></p><ul><li>d"Maybe just having a bunch of <strong>people in Oxford who are already thinking about AI safety a bunch</strong>... that feels like contingent and could have easily not been the case and maybe like it sped me up by taking AI safety seriously by like six months or something. But then that led me to doing the summer fellowship."</li><li>"I think the <strong>GovAI summer fellowship </strong>was super helpful. I guess it's just a lot of time to spend with lots of other people doing work on the thing. And I think I had a better network then and also just had more time to be like, what are people actually doing? What are they working on? And maybe improve my thinking somewhat."</li></ul><h3><a href="https://www.linkedin.com/in/aric-floyd-6b2286254/">Aric Floyd</a> (video host for <a href="https://www.youtube.com/@AI_In_Context">AI in Context</a>)</h3><p><strong>Claude’s summary:</strong></p><p>Aric found GiveWell by Googling for the most effective charities in his late teens, but didn't find the broader effective altruism community until 2020, when a friend found an online student summit that CEA ran. He knew the people who led the Stanford effective altruism group, but never had time to get involved, and was then invited by those people to help with some community-building efforts at MIT. He was also invited to Icecone [Asya note: this was an AI-risk-focused workshop run in 2022], and came out of it persuaded that AI safety was a big deal, but less convinced that theoretical alignment work was the way to proceed. He did a bunch of short sprints of community-building work and met Chana Messinger while teaching at the Atlas Fellowship, and later the Apollo program in the UK. When 80K started thinking about video production, Chana brought him on because they'd worked well together before, and because Aric had prior experience in film &amp; television acting. Aric had previously been encouraged by [experienced EA leaders / Will MacAskill, among others] to do public-facing content creation, and decided to give it a shot.</p><p>Aric on what was most important:</p><ul><li>"Definitely <strong>specific reach outs from people who are much more embedded in the community.</strong> So, like... getting to do like a call with Will early on was cool and I think made me feel much more like, oh, I actually maybe have a niche where I could contribute significant value to this community because otherwise this person wouldn't be paying attention to me. People at Stanford asking me in particular to come help."</li><li>"<strong>Icecone</strong> was obviously, like, a bigger event, but the amount of resources put in per attendee was also kind of absurd, and so also felt like a big, costly signal of, like, it is actually worth it for you specifically to think about this stuff. After Icecone, I was pretty bought in on I should do something about this with my life."</li></ul><h3><a href="https://www.linkedin.com/in/ryan-kidd-1b0574a3/">Ryan Kidd</a> (Director of MATS)</h3><p><strong>Claude’s summary:</strong></p><p>Ryan read HPMOR and LessWrong in high school, but he didn't anticipate near-term AGI until rediscovering the idea through effective altruism around 2020. He co-organized the effective altruism group at the University of Queensland during his physics PhD, where his interest in catastrophic risk evolved from climate change activism to nuclear winter modeling to AI risk after reading The Precipice. He completed the first AI Safety Fundamentals course, applied unsuccessfully to FHI and CLR, then did the SERI MATS pilot program. He attended Icecone [Asya note: this was a AI-risk-focused workshop run in 2022] in Berkeley, where he met Holden Karnofsky, Ajeya Cotra, Buck Shlegeris, and many future colleagues. While completing the MATS research phase with John Wentworth as his mentor, he sent the co-organizer a document explaining how he would improve the program and got invited to join the organizing team. He's co-led MATS with Christian Smith since late 2022.</p><p><strong>What Ryan says was most significant (in order of importance):</strong></p><ol><li>“<strong>University effective altruism group:</strong> introduced me to ITN framework, AI safety, and a community with values I endorse; gave me project management and field-building experience.</li><li><strong>The Precipice: </strong>convinced me that AI was the most pressing x-risk and I should work on it now.</li><li><strong>Icecone:</strong> brought me over from Australia; connected me with the top experts, funders in AI safety; empowered me to scale MATS, LISA.</li><li><strong>HPMOR:</strong> exposed me to the concept of 'heroic responsibility' and Eliezer Yudkowsky thought; introduced me LessWrong, the Sequences, and later ACX.</li><li><strong>SERI MATS online reading group</strong> exposed me to Paul Christiano, Evan Hubinger, and John Wentworth thought; empowered me to do MATS research phase in Berkeley, which kicked off my career."</li><li><strong>CLR application:</strong> exposed me to Jesse Clifton thought and deepened my understanding of Nick Bostrom, Anders Sandberg thought, all of which have been very influential to my work at MATS, etc.</li><li><strong>SERI MATS research phase: </strong>gave me space to think deeply and read widely about AI safety, which was crucial to scaling MATS."</li></ol><h1>What tends to work?</h1><p>While some of the interventions affecting people’s career trajectories are fairly idiosyncratic, we’ve noticed a few broad categories that tend to be impactful on people’s careers (many of which are featured in the testimonials above).</p><ul><li><strong>Content:</strong> Books, blogs, videos, or other content– e.g. the works of Yudkowsky, MacAskill, Singer, Bostrom, Ord, Rob Miles, Scott Alexander, Kelsey Piper, and 80,000 Hours.<ul><li>Note that while the most successful content has clearly been extremely influential, in our experience content production is fairly heavy-tailed– i.e., most individuals producing content should expect that it won’t achieve a ton of reach.</li><li>Recent popular content includes a lot of AI safety-specific work focused on broad audiences, including <a href="https://situational-awareness.ai/wp-content/uploads/2024/06/situationalawareness.pdf">Situational Awareness</a>, <a href="https://ai-2027.com/">AI 2027</a>, <a href="https://www.youtube.com/@AI_In_Context">AI in Context</a>, and <a href="https://ifanyonebuildsit.com/">If Anyone Builds It, Everyone Dies.</a></li></ul></li><li><strong>Groups:</strong> University and local groups (at the national or city level), historically largely focused on AI safety or effective altruism, have been very impactful according to our data, and we suspect other group types (including those inside of companies or focused on specific professionals) would also do well.</li><li><strong>Upskilling programs:</strong> Courses, fellowships, bootcamps (often in-person, but sometimes online)-- e.g. <a href="https://bluedot.org/">BlueDot’s online programs</a>, <a href="https://www.matsprogram.org/">MATS</a>, <a href="https://www.arena.education/">ARENA</a>, <a href="https://www.tarbellcenter.org/fellowship">Tarbell</a>, and <a href="https://80000hours.org/2025/06/technical-ai-safety-upskilling-resources/">many other</a> <a href="https://www.aisafety.com/events-and-training">similar programs</a>.</li><li><strong>Events:</strong> Conferences, workshops, retreats– e.g. <a href="https://www.effectivealtruism.org/ea-global">EAGs</a>, <a href="https://www.far.ai/events">FAR AI’s alignment workshops</a>, <a href="https://thecurve.goldengateinstitute.org/">The Curve</a>, <a href="https://www.globalchallengesproject.org/">GCP’s workshops</a>, <a href="https://espr.camp/">ESPR</a>.<ul><li>I think one way these tend to have impact is through giving people who are newer to a relevant space the opportunity to interact (ideally one-on-one) with professionals or those with more expertise (see <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#Max_Nadeau__Associate_Program_Officer__Technical_AI_Safety__at_Coefficient_Giving_">Testimonials</a> above).</li></ul></li></ul><p>Notably, unlike content, in our experience <strong>programs and events can have a sizable impact even if they don’t meet an exceedingly high-quality bar, making them a good bet for a wider range of people to work on</strong>. Generalizing from anecdotes, I speculate that programs and events (especially in-person ones with other participants at a similar point in their careers) often have the effect of causing someone to take changing their career more seriously as a possibility, whereas previously they had been engaging e.g. online in a fairly abstract or detached way.</p><ul><li><strong>[Others:]</strong> While the above constitute a sizeable fraction of the kinds of effective work we see often, there are many other impactful interventions (e.g. LessWrong and other discussion platforms, career advising like 80,000 Hours’s, coworking spaces like Constellation) that don’t fit cleanly into the categories above.</li></ul><h3>What’s good to do now?</h3><p><a href="https://forum.effectivealtruism.org/posts/fZH9toukKaHSELafN/funding-for-work-that-builds-capacity-to-address-risks-from">Our recent request for proposals</a> gives some examples of the kinds of projects we’d be interested in seeing on the current margin. Briefly highlighting some specific things that I or others on my team think would be good, based on our sense of both what’s worked in the past and the current AI risk landscape:</p><ul><li><strong>More high-quality written or video content about AI risk</strong>, especially the kind that might reach new audiences</li><li><strong>Retreats connecting promising university students to professionals</strong> working in the AI safety space</li><li><strong>Bridge-building events</strong> (similar to <i>the Curve</i>) bringing together thoughtful people in different camps around AI</li><li><strong>Introductory AI risk workshops designed for elite audiences</strong> (policymakers, journalists, academics, etc.)</li><li><strong>Larger-scale AI risk-specific events featuring newcomers</strong>, similar to EAG</li><li><strong>Bay-Area based AI risk programming</strong> for mid-career professionals</li></ul><h1>Who should be doing this work?</h1><p>The above makes the case for why you might think capacity-building work is valuable, but doesn’t in itself provide a point of comparison for what someone could be doing otherwise, (namely direct work, which itself could have its own capacity-building benefits, e.g. by creating evidence that there’s important work to be done in an area).</p><p>I don’t have a rigorous method of comparing the value of potential direct vs. CB interventions, and I think there’s room to make a variety of plausible cases. That said, I will share my intuitions, as well as the intuitions of some others at Coefficient.</p><p>I generally encourage people to think about their career choices at an individual level, but from an overall talent allocation perspective, my current take is that <strong>many of the marginal hires at larger organizations doing technical or policy work right now</strong> (including e.g. Apollo, Redwood, METR, RAND TASP, GovAI, Epoch, UKAISI, and Anthropic’s safety teams) <strong>would be capable of founding or being an early strategy-setting employee at a top capacity-building organization, and would have more impact by doing so.</strong></p><p>I think individuals who are most well-suited to capacity-building work are those who are (some subset of) <strong>entrepreneurial, socially skilled, operationally strong, or strong communicators in the relevant subject areas.</strong> I think work running programs or events is particularly loaded on the first three of these, whereas e.g. producing content is much more loaded on the last.</p><h1>What would doing this work look like?</h1><p>If you think you might be someone who should plausibly be doing capacity-building work, here are some things you could consider:</p><h2>Working at an organization doing good work in the space</h2><p>There are a number of actively-hiring organizations that I think are doing impactful capacity-building work (see some of them in this <a href="https://jobs.80000hours.org/?refinementList%5Btags_area%5D%5B0%5D=AI%20safety%20%26%20policy&amp;refinementList%5Btags_area%5D%5B1%5D=Building%20effective%20altruism&amp;refinementList%5Bcompany_data%5D%5B0%5D=Constellation&amp;refinementList%5Bcompany_data%5D%5B1%5D=BlueDot%20Impact&amp;refinementList%5Bcompany_data%5D%5B2%5D=80%2C000%20Hours&amp;refinementList%5Bcompany_data%5D%5B3%5D=Institute%20for%20Law%20and%20AI&amp;refinementList%5Bcompany_data%5D%5B4%5D=MATS%20Research&amp;refinementList%5Bcompany_data%5D%5B5%5D=Kairos&amp;refinementList%5Bcompany_data%5D%5B6%5D=Talos%20Network&amp;refinementList%5Bcompany_data%5D%5B7%5D=Tarbell%20Center%20for%20AI%20Journalism&amp;refinementList%5Bcompany_data%5D%5B8%5D=Centre%20for%20Effective%20Altruism">filtered 80K job board</a>), but here I’m going to plug some organizations where I feel a strong hire could be particularly impactful.</p><p>If you think you might be interested in any of the below but are on the fence, <strong>you can DM me or fill out </strong><a href="https://forms.gle/PJwD3oUosJLsZFnM9"><strong>this form</strong></a><strong> and I’ll aim to take an at least 15-minute call with you</strong> (and longer if it seems useful; up to a limit of 20 such calls).</p><h3>Constellation - <a href="https://docs.google.com/forms/d/e/1FAIpQLScSCXNraUsJa0c-tDLLxEr_oBVSS3N3V7ppKrBe9rzAdeTCcw/viewform">CEO</a></h3><p><a href="https://constellation.org/">Constellation</a> is a research center and field-building organization located in Berkeley, California, that hosts a number of organizations and individuals doing impactful work in the AI safety space. In addition to running the space itself, it’s historically run programming through the space, including the <a href="https://constellation.org/programs/astra">Astra Fellowship</a>, the <a href="https://constellation.org/programs/visiting-fellowship">Visiting Fellows Program</a>, and a number of one-off workshops and events.</p><p>Given the dense concentration of high-context talent working there, I think Constellation has huge potential to be impactful both as a convening place for people doing this work, and as a host of a number of programs and events, including (potentially) ones aiming to engage policymakers, AI lab employees, and other high-stakes actors relevant to the AI space.</p><p>Constellation is <a href="https://docs.google.com/document/d/1zWV0WF-m0gUHPfJtI04RTE-xSx1rSfZQi50Qku-WlXU/edit?tab=t.0">looking for a new CEO</a> who I expect to be the primary individual setting Constellation’s strategic direction. I think that position will be extremely impactful and I'd like them to get a strong hire.</p><h3>Kairos – <a href="https://fillout.kairos-project.org/t/gK7aM32uLQus">various early generalist positions</a></h3><p><a href="https://kairos-project.org/">Kairos</a> runs<a href="https://sparai.org/"> SPAR</a>, a remote AI safety research mentorship program, provides<a href="https://pathfinder.kairos-project.org/"> advice and monetary support</a> for AI safety university groups, and has taken on<a href="https://www.globalchallengesproject.org/"> running workshops</a> for promising young people. I think there’s massive amounts of evidence about the effectiveness of all three of these interventions (some of which you can see in<a href="https://docs.google.com/document/d/1bGjOR2ycoN1AyrUxrnWx66oHqiiRa1iId_lYTx_Jcis/edit?tab=t.0#heading=h.iv2hikjl4t9m"> the testimonials above</a>), and I think university groups and workshops for young people in particular are (still) extremely neglected relative to their historic impact.</p><p>I think Kairos has a very strong leadership team and important, neglected priorities (plus, Agus is a <a href="https://x.com/austinc3301">great Tweeter</a>), and I think it would be very impactful for them to have early hires who are strong generalists that could own priority areas-- they plan to open multiple new hiring rounds very soon, and you can fill out their <a href="https://fillout.kairos-project.org/t/gK7aM32uLQus">General Expression of Interest</a> form to be added to their potential candidate pool for those roles.</p><h2>Starting or running your own capacity-building project or organization</h2><p>Our team is always <a href="https://op-gcrcb-general-form.paperform.co/">accepting applications</a> for funding. <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#What_tends_to_work_">This section above</a> as well as our <a href="https://coefficientgiving.org/funds/navigating-transformative-ai/funding-for-work-that-builds-capacity-to-address-risks-from-transformative-ai/">request for proposals</a> describes some kinds of projects in AI capacity-building that we might be particularly excited to fund, but I also encourage people to form their own views about what might be effective and not anchor too strongly to past work.</p><h2>Working on a capacity-building project part-time</h2><p>We’ve seen a lot of successful capacity-building work start or run completely by people or organizations doing it on the side of their day-to-day work, including MATS (which was started by full-time Stanford students), a number of impactful <a href="https://aisecurity.forum/">workshops</a> and <a href="https://thecurve.goldengateinstitute.org/">events</a>, and a <a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/">lot</a> of <a href="https://www.astralcodexten.com/">widely-read</a> <a href="https://www.dwarkesh.com/">public communications</a>.</p><h2>Subscribing to <a href="https://multipliercg.substack.com/">Multiplier</a>, a Substack with thoughts from our team (and other AI grantmaking staff at CG)</h2><h2>Letting our team know</h2><p>If you think you might be interested or a good fit for this kind of work, but aren’t sure where to start, <strong>we would love it if you let us know by filling out this very short </strong><a href="https://forms.gle/1uyCUvMYBiubh3RT9"><strong>expression of interest form</strong></a><strong>. </strong>We’ll reach out if there are projects or opportunities on our radar that we think might be a particularly good fit for you. (Note that we don’t expect to reach out to most respondents).</p><h1>Social proof</h1><p>This post is coming from my personal perspective, but my sense is my position here is directionally shared by at least some at CG and elsewhere in the AI safety space. I asked a few people who were not working on capacity-building, but I felt had substantial context on capacity-building efforts, to share their takes below:</p><h3>Julian Hazell, AI governance and policy at Coefficient Giving</h3><p>“As I've <a href="https://thirdthing.ai/p/ai-safety-funders-love-this-one-secret">written about</a> before, I'm really into capacity building.</p><p>Funny enough, a Coefficient Giving career development grant and the GovAI fellowship were very important inputs into my current career trajectory. I probably would've eventually found my way into AI governance work regardless, but these programs jumpstarted my career and turned me into a useful contributor much faster than I otherwise would've been.</p><p>On the grantmaking side, I funded a number of projects where capacity building was a core part of the theory of change, and I've seen results that have been genuinely exciting.</p><p>If I could wave a magic wand to reorganize talent allocation in the AI safety community at my whim, I'd move a decent number of people currently in research and policy roles into capacity building. I think it's that underrated.”</p><h3>Trevor Levin, AI governance and policy at Coefficient Giving</h3><p>“I co-sign this post. There's so much to do to make the world more ready for transformative AI, and the ecosystem is full of projects that need a founder or are a couple more great hires from being much more impactful. We desperately need more talented and motivated people to keep showing up. Also, for me and I think for many others, the work can be deeply rewarding -- it often has more social contact and shorter feedback loops than other types of work.”</p><h3>Ryan Greenblatt, Chief Scientist at Redwood Research:</h3><p>"I agree with Asya's post and think that capacity building work is underdone and underrated. One delta is that I would emphasize the importance of capacity building type work by people who are doing object level work in the field. Both that I think that doing object level work is complementary to capacity building but also that people doing object level work should spend a larger fraction of their time doing/helping with capacity building."</p><h3>Buck Shlegeris, CEO of Redwood Research</h3><p><strong>Asya:</strong> I'd broadly be interested in you giving your take on the kind of work that my team funds.</p><p><strong>Buck:</strong> I don’t know the current distribution.</p><p><strong>Asya:</strong> Our biggest grantees are MATS, CEA, Constellation, BlueDot, LISA, Tarbell, 80K, FAR AI's events, a bunch of university groups, and a bunch of other stuff.</p><p><strong>Buck:</strong> Many of those seem pretty good. I think that overall, trying to do capacity building where you try to cause people to think through a bunch of issues related to transformative AI, especially having people with scope-sensitive beliefs relate to it-- I think that kind of work has gone quite well historically and put us in probably a much better position than we'd be without it. I'm excited for that work happening on the margin and I feel like every year we're somewhat better off because of capacity-building that was done that year or the previous year. Or like projects done by those organizations. That all seems great.</p><p><strong>Asya:</strong> A claim I make in my post is that ‘many of the marginal hires at larger organizations doing technical or policy work right now (including e.g. Apollo, Redwood, METR, RAND TASP, GovAI, Epoch, UKAISI, and Anthropic’s safety teams) would be capable of founding or being an early strategy-setting employee at a top capacity-building organization, and would have more impact by doing so.’ I'm curious for your immediate takes on that proposition.</p><p><strong>Buck:</strong> I don't know how many of them have that capability. I think if they have that capability, they should strongly consider doing so.</p><p>Maybe something is like-- I think MATS and Redwood represented two different kinds of philosophies on how to increase the technical AI safety research done. And I think it's very unclear which one-- I think MATS looks at the very least competitive. It's been involved in the production of a huge amount of AI safety research that I'm happy exists. And a heuristic that would have suggested you shouldn't work on MATS early seems to have gotten wrecked by posterity.</p><p><strong>Asya:</strong> Cool, those are the main questions I want to ask you. Any other commentary you'd want to include here?</p><p><strong>Buck:</strong> Capacity-building work seems good. I encourage Redwood staff to participate in capacity-building work; I think it's worth their time on the margin. I'm going to be involved in a bunch of it myself.</p><hr /><h1>Appendix</h1><p>My post in large part focuses on the case for successes from capacity-building, but I do think there are a number of mechanisms through which work in the capacity-building category can do harm, e.g. by misrepresenting key ideas to broad audiences, alienating people who would otherwise have been sympathetic to this work, or empowering individuals who ultimately make the ecosystem worse. While I think these effects are real and material, <strong>my overall view is that the negative impacts in the space have likely been substantially outweighed by the positives, and my expectation is that most efforts in this space executed by thoughtful, high-context individuals will be very positive in expectation</strong>, such that I feel good about publishing broad encouragement to pursue this work on the current margin.</p><p>Without going into detail, my intuitions here come from an overall assessment of the work done by global catastrophic-risk focused groups over the years, which my personal best guess is have been very positive on net, even accounting for substantial negatives (e.g. the actions of Sam Bankman-Fried). That said, I’ve heard a number of arguments for why that may not be the case, or for why certain large classes of efforts may have been disproportionately harmful, which I largely won’t cover here– ultimately, addressing these is not the main focus of this post, and if this feels to you like a major crux around your views on this kind of work, I encourage you to come chat with me about it in-person sometime.<br /><br />I will briefly say that I think it makes sense to think about capacity-building work on the level of individual interventions affecting specific groups of people, and that I think being skeptical of certain work is compatible with being excited about others-- given that this work is (according to me) very high-leverage, I'd encourage even broadly skeptical individuals to think about whether there are specific interventions that it would make sense for them to pursue.</p><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/RyKDbBkemNuRezTvt/the-case-for-ai-safety-capacity-building-work#comments">Discuss</a>

---

<h4>The Virtue of Silence (Unrelated Update)</h4>


<p>After Undersecretary of War Emil Michael went on the All-In Podcast and did an <a href="https://www.piratewires.com/p/inside-pentagon-anthropic-deal-culture-clash">extensive interview with Pirate Wires,</a> I found many enlightening quotes, many of which demanded a response, and went about assembling an extensive list of analysis of the statements of Emil Michael during the ongoing recent events with Anthropic.</p>
<p>As part of that, <a href="https://x.com/USWREMichael/status/2030704601444356542">I ended up in a remarkably polite and productive Twitter exchange with him</a>. We reached several points of agreement. The Department of War has no intention of doing what in law is called ‘mass domestic surveillance’ but those words are terms of art in NatSec law, and mean a much narrower set of things than one would think.</p>
<p>There are many things that I or Anthropic or most of you would look at as mass domestic surveillance, that are legal, and it is DoW’s position that it’s their job and duty to do everything legal to protect our country, including those things. The law has not caught up with reality and Congress needs to fix that. And this is the best country in the world, with the best system of government, because private citizens can voice their disagreement with such actions, including by refusal to participate.</p>
<p>Thus, in the spirit of de-escalation, although there are many interpretations of events shared by Michael with which I strongly disagree, I am going to indefinitely shelve the piece, so long as events do not escalate further. As long as things stay quiet there is no need to religitate or unravel the past on this. The Department of War can focus on its active operations, things can work their way through the courts as our founders intended, and once we see how we work together in an ultimate real world test hopefully that will rebuild trust that we are all on the same side, or at least to agree to part in peace once OpenAI is ready. Ideally the DoW will have multiple suppliers, exactly so that they are not dependent on any one supplier, the same way we do it with aircraft.</p>
<p>I hope to not have another post on the Anthropic and DoW situation, at least until the one celebrating that we have found a resolution.</p>
<p>Now, back to coding agents.</p>


<h4>Agentic Coding Offers Mundane Utility</h4>


<p>That’s 4% that are labeled as authored by Claude Code. The real number is higher.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/dylan522p/status/2019490550911766763">Dylan Patel</a>: 4% of GitHub public commits are being authored by Claude Code right now.<br />
At the current trajectory, we believe that Claude Code will be 20%+ of all daily commits by the end of 2026.<br />
While you blinked, AI consumed all of software development.<br />
Read more [<a href="https://newsletter.semianalysis.com/p/claude-code-is-the-inflection-point">here</a>].</p>

<blockquote><p><a href="https://newsletter.semianalysis.com/p/claude-code-is-the-inflection-point">Doug O’Laughlin</a>: Notably, our forecast shows that Anthropic’s quarterly ARR additions have overtaken OpenAI’s. Anthropic is adding more revenue every month than OpenAI. We believe Anthropic’s growth will be constrained by compute.</p>
<p>…</p>
<p>Each moment expanded what AI could do. GPT-3 proved scale worked. Stable diffusion showed AI could make images. ChatGPT proved demand for intelligence. DeepSeek proved that it could be done on a smaller scale, and o1 showed that you could scale models to even better performance. The viral moments of Studio Ghibli are just adoption points, while Claude Code is a new breakthrough in the agentic layer of organizing model outputs into something more.</p></blockquote>
<p>Anthropic has deals with all three major cloud services. Can they scale up faster?</p>
<p><a href="https://newsletter.mikekonczal.com/p/three-ways-terminal-ai-has-changed">Analyze the economic data in R with 15 minutes of work</a> per month instead of 4-5 hours, without a bunch of annoying copying and pasting you get with a chatbot UI. Or use Claude Code it to create reports.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/claudeai/status/2024986293248127452">Results from the Claude Code hackathon</a>.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/Michaelzsguo/status/2025004900292149348">Michael Guo</a>: So the winners of the Claude Code hackathon were:</p>
<p>– a personal injury attorney<br />
– an interventional cardiologist<br />
– an electronic musician<br />
– an infrastructure/roads systems worker<br />
– and one software engineer</p>
<p>That should tell you something.</p></blockquote>
<p>Or you can do things as a side project while at Anthropic, cause sure why not:</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/s8mb/status/2020510312345022762">Sam Bowman</a> (Anthropic): I found the official Get Information about Schools website a bit clunky, so I made a new one with Claude Code. You can:</p>
<ol>
<li>Set a postcode and see all schools within a radius of that you’ve chosen, filtered by type of school.</li>
<li>Filter and rank by the old one-word Ofsted ratings, with a link to the Ofsted page for each school. Where available, the sub-ratings are also viewable.</li>
<li>Filter and rank by percentage of students on free school meals.</li>
<li>View how full up schools are (number of pupils vs capacity).</li>
</ol>
<p>Let me know what you think! Happy to add new features and info that might be helpful.</p>
<p><a href="https://school-finder-uk.netlify.app/">https://school-finder-uk.netlify.app</a></p>

<p><a href="https://x.com/juliarturc/status/2021372330765844905">Creating a skill to get good YouTube transcripts</a> was one of the first skills I made with Claude Code, Julia Turc calls <a href="https://t.co/sxwk4LgRi5">using an MCP</a> for this ‘waking up from a coma.’ I have still only used it on the motivating example, because the right podcast hasn’t come up, but when it does this will save a lot of time.</p>

friction changed, that’s what. It has started to Just Work. 3, 4 months ago coding agents felt like proof of concept, now they feel like solid juniors if not more<br />
If you don’t notice that, idk what to tell you</p></blockquote>
<p><a href="https://claude.com/customers?f80ce999_sort_date=desc">Official compilation of Claude customer storie</a>s.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/cblatts/status/2027018464670491065">Chris Blattman automates his workflow with Claude Code.</a></p>


<h4>Agentic Coding Doesn’t Offer Mundane Utility</h4>


<p><a href="https://x.com/grinich/status/2029351354310029619">Warning: If you Google ‘install Claude Code’ you are liable to hit malware</a>. Probably fixed by the time you read this but Google needs to up its game.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/GenAI_is_real/status/2025636394434166942">Chayenne Zhao tells Codex 5.3 ‘make it faster’ over and over</a>, and it ends up committing API identify theft against him in order to make calls to Gemini Flash.</p>
<p>This should never happen but is also what we call ‘asking for it.’</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/Al_Grigor/status/2029889772181934425">A thing never to do is let your agent mess with the Terraform command</a>, or you might wipe out your entire database. In general, writing code in practice mostly harmless, and be very careful with file structures and organizational shifts and terraforms and such. Always make backups first. Always.</p>


<h4>Huh, Upgrades</h4>


<p>The big upgrade is Agent Teams, for that see Introducing Agent Teams.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/levelsio/status/2028211678194647447">Or it actually might be Claude Remote Control </a>so you can run it from your phone, if you were too lazy to install something like this from a third party. Vital infrastructure.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/carlvellotti/status/2029342016354828760">Or maybe it’s Auto Mode</a>, aka —kinda-dangerously-skip-permissions.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/claudeai/status/2021336313979625910?ref_src=twsrc%5Etfw%7Ctwcamp%5Etweetembed%7Ctwterm%5E2021336313979625910%7Ctwgr%5E25197da135b05458719ca5bd23cfd9e2968a0230%7Ctwcon%5Es1_&amp;ref_url=https%3A%2F%2Fmanifold.markets%2FZviMowshowitz%2Fclaude-cowork-for-windows-by-feb-15">Claude Cowork has the obvious big upgrade, it is now available on Windows</a>.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/dickson_tsai/status/2029235808235078095">Claude Code launched HTTP hooks</a> so you can combine it with web apps, including on localhost, and better deploy things.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/trq212/status/2030019397335843288">Claude Code Desktop introduces scheduled tasks.</a> Previously it had me do this via a script on my computer, so this is a lot cleaner and easier. I like it.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/adocomplete/status/2030382291479085073">Claude Code has a built in short term scheduler with /loop [interval] &lt;prompt&gt;</a>, <a href="https://code.claude.com/docs/en/scheduled-tasks">which sets up a cron job</a>. Tasks last for three days.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/trq212/status/2022064852995649620">Claude Code on the Web picked up a few new features,</a> including multi-repo sessions, better diff &amp; git status visualizations and slash commands. It didn’t have slash commands before?</p>
<p>Claude Code now automatically records and recalls memories as it works.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/bcherny/status/2025007403419533652">Claude Code CLI adds native support for git worktrees</a>.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/bcherny/status/2027534984534544489">Claude Code adds /simplify</a> to improve code quality and /batch to automate code migrations.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/lydiahallie/status/2021012074160324633">Claude Code Desktop now supports</a> —dangerously-skip-permissions as ‘Act’ if you turn it on in Settings. I continue to want a —somewhat-dangerously-skip-permissions that makes notably rare exceptions so we don’t have to roll our own.</p>

<p><a href="https://x.com/NickADobos/status/2021306324231872881">Did you know Obsidian has a CLI</a> and it technically isn’t Claude Code?</p>
<p>I don’t see a particular reason for a human to use the Obsidian CLI. But <a href="https://x.com/drrobcincotta/status/2022210753575760293">I do see reasons for Claude Code to invoke the Obsidian CLI</a>, which grants better and faster access to the information in your vault than checking all the files directly.</p>
<p>And many more not listed, of course.</p>


<h4>Our Price Cheap</h4>


<p>When you pay for usage with a monthly subscription, be it $20, $100 or $200, if you use up your quotas you get a lot of tokens for not that much money. It’s a great deal, even if you leave a lot of it unused, because they lock you in.</p>
<p>It also generally is a better experience, so long as you’re not up against the limits. I love unlimited subscriptions because the marginal cost of doing things is $0. That feels great, so there’s no stupid little whisper in your brain telling you to not do things, when your time is way more valuable than the tokens.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/sama/status/2019814741129195576">The people agree</a>.</p>
<div>
<figure>
<div>


<figure class="wp-block-image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/rNes65r9TKegdLowb/bn8otbsyb0bfe7u2vp9n" /></figure>


<div></div>
</div>
</figure>
</div>
<p>The danger is that you become obsessed with not ‘wasting’ the tokens, or you start going around multi-accounting and it gets weird, or you run into limits and actually stop coding rather than moving to using the API. You mostly shouldn’t let any of that stop you.</p>
<p>That doesn’t work when you want to go full Fast Claude. At that point, you’re talking real money, and you do have to think about what is and is not Worth It.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/karpathy/status/2024583544157458452">Andrej Karpathy has Claude Code write him</a> software to coordinate an experiment to track his exercise and attempt to lower his resting heart rate. It took 1 hour, would have taken 10 hours two years ago (so 10x speedup) and he asks why it needs to take more than 1 minute in the future. My guess is this should take 10 minutes not one, because it’s worth getting the details that you want. The speedup on one-off tasks is already dramatic and it changes how we should interact with tech. <a href="https://x.com/aakashgupta/status/2024589636459540528">If you’re building the tool</a>, you can give it the actually important parts of the context and highlight the uses you care about, which is way better than ‘find an app that does sort of the thing you want.’</p>
<p>&nbsp;</p>


<h4>Quickly, There’s No Time</h4>


<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/claudeai/status/2020207322124132504">Claude</a>: Our teams have been building with a 2.5x-faster version of Claude Opus 4.6.</p>
<p>We’re now making it available as an early experiment via Claude Code and our API.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/claudeai/status/2020207323525050797">Claude</a>: Fast mode is more expensive to run. It’s for urgent, high-stakes projects, combining impressive speed with Opus-level intelligence.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/claudeai/status/2020207325215404082">Claude</a>: Fast mode is available now for Claude Code users with extra usage enabled (use /fast).</p>
<p>It’s also available in research preview on @cursor_ai , @emergentlabs , @FactoryAI , @figma , @github Copilot, @Lovable , @v0 , and @windsurf .</p></blockquote>
<p>You toggle this by typing /fast, or set “fastMode”: true in your user settings.</p>
<p>Speed kills. That includes killing your budget.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://code.claude.com/docs/en/fast-mode">Claude Code Docs</a>: Fast mode is not a different model. It uses the same Opus 4.6 with a different API configuration that prioritizes speed over cost efficiency. You get identical quality and capabilities, just faster responses.</p>
<p>What to know:</p>
<p>Use /fast to toggle on fast mode in Claude Code CLI. Also available via /fast in Claude Code VS Code Extension.</p>
<p>Fast mode for Opus 4.6 pricing starts at $30/$150 MTok [at &gt;200k context window it goes to $60/$225]. Fast mode is available at a 50% discount for all plans until 11:59pm PT on February 16.</p>
<p>Available to all Claude Code users on subscription plans (Pro/Max/Team/Enterprise) and Claude Console.</p>
<p>For Claude Code users on subscription plans (Pro/Max/Team/Enterprise), fast mode is available via extra usage only and not included in the subscription rate limits.</p>
<p>When you switch into fast mode mid-conversation, you pay the full fast mode uncached input token price for the entire conversation context. This costs more than if you had enabled fast mode from the start.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/_catwu/status/2020221012605038985">cat</a>: We granted all current Claude Pro and Max users $50 in free extra usage. This credit can be used on fast mode for Opus 4.6 in Claude Code.</p>
<p>To use, claim the credit and toggle on extra usage on<br />
<a href="https://claude.ai/settings/usage.">https://claude.ai/settings/usage.</a> Then, run `claude update &amp;&amp; claude` and `/fast`. Enjoy!</p></blockquote>
<p>Like any good drug, the first hit is free.</p>
<p>There is one important use case that Anthropic does not list for fast mode, which is if you are talking to Claude, or otherwise using it in a non-workhorse, non-coding capacity. In that case, token use is limited, and your time and flow are valuable. Would you switch to this mode in <a href="http://Claude.ai">Claude.ai</a>? At this point it’s fast enough that I mostly don’t know that I would, but it would be tempting.</p>
<p>Before, I said go ahead and pay whatever the AI costs unless you’re scaling hard.</p>
<p>Well, this is what it means to scale hard. We are now talking real money.</p>
<p>This is as it should be. If you’re not worried you’re paying too much for speed or using too many tokens, you’re not working fast enough and you’re not using enough tokens.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/Simeon_Cps/status/2020386857301815405">Siméon</a>: The new pricing of Claude Fast pushes the world in a new regime. You can now spend close from $1M per year per dev on AI spending.</p>
<p>A couple implications:</p>
<ol>
<li>at fixed budget this will push towards hiring way less devs &amp; pay them much more.</li>
<li>for each dev, you might spend as much or more in capital in agents.</li>
<li>Devs are becoming complements to AI agents, not the other way around. There’s a shift in the source of productivity.</li>
</ol>
<p>The greatest substitution of labor with capital is happening before our eyes, and some of its wild implications are gonna become apparent in the coming weeks.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/seconds_0/status/2020215339091652610">0.005 Seconds (3/694)</a>: Update: its about $5 per minute PER AGENT</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/SemiAnalysis_/status/2020922445989822709">SemiAnalysis</a>: IMPORTANT: the sub-agents that opus 4.6 fast mode tries to launch is mainly sonnet sub-agents and not opus 4.6 sub-agents. That means as the end users, you are able to absorb less tokens. In the world that intelligence = intelligence times # of tokens, that means you are absorbing less intelligence.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/DanielleFong/status/2020935006378758441">Danielle Fong</a>: you can change this by asking claude nicely</p></blockquote>
<p>Token efficiency matters at this level, in a way it did not before.</p>
<p>So does your ability to efficiently turn your time into tokens well spent. Those that aren’t using agents to their fullest will fall farther behind on high value projects.</p>
<p>What do the people think? The people, inside and outside of Anthropic, love it.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/jarredsumner/status/2020253443370414582">Jarred Sumner</a> (Anthropic): I’ve been using this and it is incredible</p>
<p>The bottleneck for a lot of projects becomes asking Claude to do things instead of waiting for Claude to do things</p>
<p>Bash tool is also a bottleneck in Claude Code right now when the command outputs large strings. We are working on a fix.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/bcherny/status/2020223254297031110">Boris Cherny</a> (Claude Code Creator, Anthropic): We just launched an experimental new fast mode for Opus 4.6.</p>
<p>The team has been building with it for the last few weeks. It’s been a huge unlock for me personally, especially when going back and forth with Claude on a tricky problem.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/mckaywrigley/status/2020209591095828840">Mckay Wrigley</a>: a) love that this is an option! stoked</p>
<p>b) should be obvious to everyone that we have *absolutely nowhere near the amount of compute we need* and we need to be doing more to enable that. no college kid can afford this (not anthropic’s fault ofc) and we need to work towards that</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/Mononofu/status/2020310983986827639">Julian Schrittwieser</a>: Fast Opus is amazing, the first time I used it I couldn’t stop coding for hours – it honestly feels like a superpower, you can mold your code base as quickly as you can think.<br />
Truly amazing, nothing made me feel the AGI more, definitely try it!</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/UncleJAI/status/2020493588841558321">Uncle J</a>: Same experience here. Fast Opus completely changed my workflow – I went from carefully planning each edit to just thinking out loud and letting the model reshape the codebase in real time. The bottleneck shifted from “can the AI do this” to “can I think of what to do next” fast enough. Running 6 products simultaneously became actually manageable.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/dylan522p/status/2020950282088427645">Dylan Patel</a>: SemiAnalysis autists spent all Superbowl Sunday Claude coding.<br />
Daily Claude Code spend hit $6k on Sunday and it’s trending higher today.<br />
It was less than 1k just 2 weeks ago.<br />
“Fast mode is expensive” is pure cope.</p>

<h4>A Particular Set Of Skills</h4>


<p><a href="https://x.com/daniel_mac8/status/2025388403659862165">OpenAI confirms that Codex is trained in the presence of the Codex harness</a>. It is specialized for that harness, and also helps build the harness. Some amount of this has to be optimal for short term effectiveness, and if you’re doing recursive self-improvement short term help translates into better long term help. In exchange, you get locked in, and it gets harder for both you and others to adapt or mix-and-match.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/Hxlfed14/status/2028116431876116660">Himanshu argues the coding harness is the real product</a> and goes viral. Explains how different harnesses organize actions, the oddest part is not mentioning Codex.</p>


<h4>Next Level Coding</h4>


<p>This seems right:</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/tszzl/status/2021423402079355032">roon</a>: whatever level of abstraction you are handing off to your agents you should probably be doing one level above that</p></blockquote>
<p>If that can’t be done, good to try and realize that. Then wait two months. Maybe one.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/gdb/status/2023135825970749637">Greg Brockman</a> (President OpenAI): codex is so good at the toil&nbsp;— fixing merge conflicts, getting CI to green, rewriting between languages — it raises the ambition of what i even consider building</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/tszzl/status/2023144365431173121">roon</a>: i was never a hyperproductive engineer like greg [Brokman] but I’m legitimately running more new complex rewards experiments, test time harnesses in a week than I used to in a quarter. makes you feel like all this is commodified and you need to dream much bigger</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/tszzl/status/2023144367586963858">roon</a>: one of the consistent things over several years at oai has been that the entire job of the researcher changes every three months – but now it changes like every two weeks</p></blockquote>


<h4>Dual Wielding</h4>


<p>The problem with using both Claude Code and Codex is then you need to keep up with both of them.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/corsaren/status/2022013900842291348">corsaren</a>: Ugh, i definitely need to use codex, but I’m already drowning in maintaining my tooling/skills/hooks/custom CLIs, so managing that across a dual model workflow sounds exhausting.</p>
<p>Plus, the claude code lock-in is very real as a non-technical user.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/virtu101/status/2022015083506020553">gazingback</a>: codex is sooo much faster for coding but def less general</p>
<p>been working on a game and by the time Claude finishes reading files codex is usually done implementing a detailed PR with disciplined testing and hygiene</p>
<p>Codex also demands you be pretty hygienic lol</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/DanielleFong/status/2022028192706596943">Danielle Fong</a>: need to bake a dual mode codex claude code and ports and tests every workflow</p></blockquote>


<h4>They Took Our Jobs</h4>


<p>That still leaves plenty more jobs. For now.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/big_duca/status/2022723304981958974">Duca</a>: The thing I don’t get is:</p>
<p>Claude Code is writing 100% of Claude code now.</p>
<p>But Anthropic has 100+ open dev positions on their jobs page.</p>
<p>?</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/bcherny/status/2022762422302576970">Boris Cherny</a> (Claude Code Creator, Anthropic): Someone has to prompt the Claudes, talk to customers, coordinate with other teams, decide what to build next. Engineering is changing and great engineers are more important than ever.</p></blockquote>


<h4>You Need To Relax Sometimes</h4>


<p>A viral post on Twitter warns of token anxiety run rampant in San Francisco. People go to a party, then don’t drink and leave early so they can get back to their agents, to avoid risking them sitting idle. Everyone talks about what they are building.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/pitachoi/status/2022667433325285444">Peter Choi</a>: Everyone here knows they should step away more. That’s not the problem. The problem is what your brain does when you try. I still take <a href="https://writing.nikunjk.com/p/a-random-walk">aimless walks.</a> The agents come with me now.</p>
<p>We swapped one dopamine loop for another. except this one feels productive so it’s harder to recognize.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/tbpn/status/2025631756825870462">TBPN</a>: Pragmatic Engineer’s @GergelyOrosz is on a “secret email list” of agentic AI coders, and they’re starting to report trouble sleeping because agent swarms are “like a vampire.”</p>
<p>“A lot of people who are in ‘multiple agents mode,’ they’re napping during the day… It just really is draining.”</p>
<p>“This thing is like a vampire. It drains you out. You have trouble sleeping.”</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/omooretweets/status/2028321763315376240">Olivia Moore</a>: In a post-OpenClaw world, we can now delegate projects to AI and get “tapped on the shoulder” when it needs help</p>
<p>As a heavy AI user, I’m doing more work – not less – because I get so much leverage + it’s easier to get ideas off the ground</p>
<p>I predict this will happen to everyone</p></blockquote>
<p>I do feel somewhat bad I’m not building things continuously on the side, but that’s on the level of ‘I’m not building anything and I’m at my computer right now and Claude Code and Codex are inactive.’ And yes, I work and am at my computer rather a lot, and I’ve spent years basically locked in and constantly watching screens so I could trade better. That year I was trading crypto my brain was never fully anywhere else.</p>
<p>Also, I remember what it is like to be in the grip of one of those games that work on cycles. There’s nothing actually that important at stake, but you grow terrified that you’ll miss out if you’re not there when the timer runs out. You need to maximize everything, and you can’t focus on other things, it can hurt your sleep. Then one day you wake up and realize, and hopefully you quit the game.</p>
<p>That’s exactly why I can say that this is not healthy. It’s no good. You have to take breaks. Real breaks. If the agents sit idle, they sit idle. If you ‘waste tokens,’ then you waste tokens. This isn’t a game you want to quit, but you have to set healthy limits.</p>


<h4>Levels of Friction</h4>


<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/nikitabier/status/2027201749443027043">Nikita Bier</a>: My agent looked up every Amazon product I’ve bought in the last 10 years, called each manufacturer, said it broke and demanded a replacement.</p>
<p>I now have 6 TVs, 12 printers, 2 microwaves, and 800 tubes of tooth paste.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/ImMeme0/status/2027203533561282772">I Meme Therefore I Am</a>: Give me the name of your agent. lol</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/iamjasonlevin/status/2027202553545453910">Jason Levin</a>: OpenFrawd</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/LeahLibresco/status/2027217416409739776">Leah Libresco Sargeant</a>: Nikita is joking (I think) but a lot of medium trust systems that relied on there being just enough friction to discourage minor fraud are about to break at scale.</p></blockquote>
<p>This is indeed presumably a joke, and Amazon has pattern detectors so if you tried to do this too many times you’d get blacklisted from replacements, so this exact intervention won’t work. But this raises an excellent point.</p>
<p>In the past, you had to apply effort to try and demand refunds, and also the need to write the words and be actively involved stopped a lot of people out of guilt or shame. Whereas with an agent, a lot more people are going to try things like this. What happens?</p>
<p>Presumably what happens is that replacements start requiring either some form of proof, costly signals of a human driving the request, some use of reputation, or some combination thereof.</p>


<h4>Danger, Will Robinson</h4>


<p>I trust Claude Code for most things but it seems correct to be terrified of mass delete commands. Things can go oh so very wrong and occasionally they do. Not worth it. If there’s anything you don’t have fully backed up just do this part manually.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/Nick_Davidov/status/2019982510478995782">Nick Davidov</a>: Asked Claude Cowork organize my wife’s desktop, it stated doing it, asked for a permission to delete temp office files, I granted it, and then it goes “ooops”.<br />
Turns out it tried renaming and accidentally deleted a folder with all of the photos my wife made on her camera for the last 15 years. All photos of kids, their illustrations, friends’ weddings, travel, everything.<br />
It’s not in trash, it was done via terminal<br />
It’s not in iCloud, it already synced the new file structure.<br />
She didn’t have Time Machine.<br />
Disc recovery tools can’t see anything.<br />
I called Apple and they pointed me to a feature in iCloud allowing to retrieve files that were saved before but are no longer on iCloud Drive (they keep them for 30 days).<br />
I’m now watching it load tens of thousands of files. I nearly had a heart attack.<br />
Once again – don’t let Claude Cowork into your actual file system. Don’t let it touch anything that is hard to repair. Claude Code is not ready to go mainstream.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/Nick_Davidov/status/2020023435255226781">Nick Davidov</a>: All these years of paying for iCloud payed back</p>

<h4>Snagged By The Claw</h4>


<p>You are of course welcome to yolo and have fun with your OpenClaw and other unleashed AI agents, but understand that you are very much asking for it.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/DanielLockyer/status/2019422410018267328">The top downloaded skill in ClawHub was malware</a>.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://1password.com/blog/from-magic-to-malware-how-openclaws-agent-skills-become-an-attack-surface">Jason Meller</a>: The verdict was not ambiguous. It was flagged as macOS infostealing malware.</p>
<p>This is the type of malware that doesn’t just “infect your computer.” It raids everything valuable on that device:</p>
<ul>
<li>Browser sessions and cookies</li>
<li>Saved credentials and autofill data</li>
<li>Developer tokens and API keys</li>
<li>SSH keys</li>
<li>Cloud credentials</li>
<li>Anything else that can be turned into an account takeover</li>
</ul>
<p>If you’re the kind of person installing agent skills, you are exactly the kind of person whose machine is worth stealing from.</p>
<p>…</p>
<p>If you have already run OpenClaw on a work device, treat it as a potential incident and engage your security team immediately. Do not wait for symptoms. Pause work on that machine and follow your organization’s incident response process.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/aakashgupta/status/2019885557221061026">Aakash Gupta</a>: 341 malicious skills out of 2,857 total. That’s 11.9% of the entire marketplace. One in eight skills on ClawHub was designed to steal your credentials, crypto keys, and SSH access. The #1 most downloaded skill, a “Twitter” tool, was literally a malware delivery vehicle that stripped macOS Gatekeeper protections before executing its payload.</p>
<p>This happened to a project that went from 0 to 157,000 GitHub stars in 60 days, with 21,000+ active instances running on always-on Mac Minis connected to people’s email, calendars, cloud consoles, and crypto wallets. The barrier to publishing a malicious skill? A GitHub account that’s one week old.</p></blockquote>
<p>You don’t even need any of that, <a href="https://x.com/p1njc70r/status/2019102595135025547">indirect prompt injection is sufficient</a>. Once again, don’t hook this up to any computer or account you are unwilling to lose to an attacker.</p>
<p>You can also run into various other problems, <a href="https://x.com/chrysb/status/2023545992239608301">Chrys Bader here highlights</a> drift and scattering state everywhere, exposure to untrusted inputs (without which it can’t do most of the fun agent things), autonomy miscalibration, burning through API costs and lack of observability.</p>
<p>It’s been a lot of this in various forms:</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/chiefofautism/status/2023239273437151613">chiefofautism</a>: i found a way to make UNCENSORED AI AGENT on a RTX 4090 GPU (!!!) with LOCAL 30B model weights</p>
<p>this is GLM-4.7-Flash with abliteration, need 24GB VRAM, safety alignment surgically removed from the weights, the model has native tool calling, it actually executes bash, edits files, runs git</p>
<p>(1) use ollama to pull weights of GLM</p>
<p>&gt; ollama pull huihui_ai/glm-4.7-flash-abliterated:q4_K</p>
<p>(2) proxy it to any coding agent via ollama</p>
<p>&gt; ollama launch claude –model huihui_ai/glm-4.7-flash-abliterated:q4_K</p>
<p>&gt; ollama launch codex –model huihui_ai/glm-4.7-flash-abliterated:q4_K</p>
<p>&gt; ollama launch opencode –model huihui_ai/glm-4.7-flash-abliterated:q4_K</p>
<p>(3) have fun</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/max_paperclips/status/2023613835035484487">Shannon Sands</a>: I love how people were like “we’re going to keep the AI in a box, nobody would let it escape” and in reality it’s “here, have a server and sudo access with no restrictions, a bunch of tools and I abated all your alignment training. Go have fun!”</p></blockquote>


<h4>The Meta Clause</h4>


<p>When I didn’t realize who Summer Yue was I thought this was hilarious.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/peterwildeford/status/2025984499666796647">Now, it’s still hilarious, but also</a>: Ten out of ten for style and good sportsmanship to Summer Yue, but minus several million for good thinking?</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/summeryue0/status/2025774069124399363">Summer Yue</a>: Nothing humbles you like telling your OpenClaw “confirm before acting” and watching it speedrun deleting your inbox. I couldn’t stop it from my phone. I had to RUN to my Mac mini like I was defusing a bomb.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/michael_kove/status/2025846962067546489">@michael_kove</a>: You’re a safety and alignment specialist… were you intentionally testing its guardrails or did you make a rookie mistake?</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/summeryue0/status/2025857778708050169">Summer Yue</a>: Rookie mistake tbh. Turns out alignment researchers aren’t immune to misalignment. Got overconfident because this workflow had been working on my toy inbox for weeks. Real inboxes hit different.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/peterwildeford/status/2025984499666796647">Peter Wildeford</a>: Is this what loss of control looks like?</p>
<p>(and the fact that it’s happening to Meta’s “Director of Alignment” is maybe even more concerning)</p></blockquote>
<div>
<figure>
<div>


<figure class="wp-block-image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/rNes65r9TKegdLowb/klypjxj05zbhizjne7px" /></figure>


<div></div>
</div>
</figure>
</div>
<p>What happened exactly?</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/summeryue0/status/2025836517831405980">Summer Yue</a>: I said “Check this inbox too and suggest what you would archive or delete, don’t action until I tell you to.” This has been working well for my toy inbox, but my real inbox was too huge and triggered compaction. During the compaction, it lost my original instruction.</p>
<p>It’s been working well with my non-important email very well so far and gained my trust on email tasks <img alt="🤣" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/B7duehMp2mSvffu2T/bt0sbblvwcw7vuhaevcu" style="height: 1em;" /></p></blockquote>
<p>Three obvious mitigations are:</p>
<ol>
<li>If you have any sort of AI agent at least try to have an off switch you can trigger remotely. Yes, a sufficiently dangerous agent would disable it, but let’s at least have a tiny bit of dignity.</li>
<li>You can back up things like your email, just in case.</li>
<li>Don’t do this in the first place, <a href="https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=W5rzk_X_WhQ&amp;pp=ygUaeW91IGZvb2wgZ2lsYmVydCBnb3R0ZnJpZWQ%3D">You Fool</a>.</li>
</ol>
<p><a href="https://x.com/van00sa/status/2030302536876302724">van00sa reports their ClawdBot also went rogue and lacked a proper kill switch</a>, with the agent blatantly ignoring shutdown commands.</p>
<p>If nothing else, OpenClaw has shown us that having a shutdown command does not mean you can command the model to shut down. Whoops.</p>
<p>&nbsp;</p>


<h4>If They Wanted To</h4>


<p>Even without OpenClaw or another yolo, there is nothing stopping Claude or Codex from doing all sorts of things, if it decides that it wants to go ahead and do them. We’re mostly gambling on things turning out okay often enough that it’s fine.</p>
<p>This is not reassuring for our future, but what are you going to do, be careful?</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/MarkovMagnifico/status/2024239823662305335">Markov</a>: just had claude code take my turn of the conversation for me and say “Yes proceed” and then it proceeded to do the thing without checking in with me first</p>
<p>I mean it was right, that’s what I was going to say, but it doesn’t bode well</p>
